#112. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#110. AI datacentres plan on-site power stations on huge scale despite climate risks, research reveals

181GW of gas-fired capacity proposed for sites in US and Europe because of delays in connecting to national grids, says report

Developers of AI datacentres plan to build on-site climate-destroying gas power stations on a huge scale to meet their energy demands, research reveals.

Datacentres planned across the US, the UK and mainland Europe have proposals to build fossil fuel plants on-site to create 181GW of gas-fired capacity because of delays to connect to national grids, according to the findings. This is almost four times the total peak electricity demand of Great Britain.

The research by BloombergNEF says this “speed to power” will – if the planned rollout happens – emit about 345m metric tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent a year in the US, where 90% of on-site gas-fired power stations are planned. This is equivalent to annual greenhouse gas emissions from about 72.8m fossil fuel cars.

The report was published on Wednesday as global leaders gathered in Turkey for the 31st UN climate summit, where increasing electrification – ideally from clean sources – is a central part of discussions. However, a number of key European leaders, including from France and Germany, will not be at Cop31, instead heading to Dublin where EU leaders will hold their first dedicated AI summit on Friday.

Mark Daly, the head of datacentres and emerging tech at BloombergNEF, said: “The extraordinary economics around AI are making speed to power one of the most important priorities for defining constraints on datacentre developers.

“The climate outcome will depend not only on how much electricity these facilities use, but on how quickly grids can connect them and whether power systems add cleaner generation alongside that demand.”

By 2035, the electricity demand of AI datacentres was predicted to almost quadruple to 2,354 terawatt-hours – equivalent to 7% of global electricity demand – the report said.

AI datacentres use a massive amount of electricity to power servers and cool equipment, and to run facility systems.

Daly said while the developers of the future datacentres claimed their gas-fired power supplies would be temporary while they waited for grid connections, once they were online, the economics could encourage them to continue generating energy, creating a longer-term fossil-fuel lock-in risk.

The report said ensuring new datacentres were connected to the grid was the more sustainable option. Faster grid connections were crucial to limiting emissions impacts and locking in fossil fuel risk, it said.

UK planned datacentres made more than 100 inquiries in 2024-5 to connect to gas as a result of delays in securing access to the electricity grid, according to Future Energy Networks, the representative body for the UK gas networks.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

For example, Wapseys Wood, Buckinghamshire and Quest Park, Bedfordshire are seeking to build their own on-site gas-fired power stations because of the long wait to connect to the grid. The datacentres are among the first to be fast-tracked through the government’s nationally significant infrastructure project regime, which allows developers to rapidly deliver major infrastructure, bypassing locally elected planning authorities.

But the use of fossil fuel power generation on site is likely to be repeated by many more UK sites after the chancellor, John Healey, gave the green light for datacentres to build and run temporary on-site fossil fuel power stations while they wait for connections to the grid, as part of a so-called datacentre covenant.

Oliver Hayes, the head of policy and campaigns at Global Action Plan , said: “Private gas power stations for datacentres should be a red line for any government serious about climate action, so it’s shocking to see the chancellor give them his explicit backing.

“No one seriously believes these gas power stations will be temporary – once they’re built, they’ll lock in high emissions for years.”

#109. Polish unity on show as thousands join silent march for Krakow shooting victims

Thousands gathered at Krakow's historic Market Square to honour the victims of Saturday's attack

Thousands of people joined a silent march through central Krakow on Monday night, in memory of those killed and injured when a gunman went on a violent rampage at the weekend.

The crowd were led by Poland's president and prime minister, the bitter political rivals putting on a rare show of unity after the country's deadliest attack in modern times.

As they reached the historic Market Square, a bugler played from the tower.

Many people were carrying flowers or candles to leave at the shrines that now dot the cobblestones, each one marking a spot where someone died.

Fiercely proud of its reputation for safety, Poland has been profoundly shaken by this attack. It has left people struggling to make some kind of sense of what happened.

The alleged gunman has been named as 24-year-old Michal Oksinski by local media.

Critically injured in a shoot-out with police, he did not regain consciousness and died in hospital early on Monday.

Initially, the interior minister claimed that Oksinski was driven by far-right, extremist views. The national prosecutor's office now says that it is too soon to say he was "inspired" by any ideology.

"The minister wanted to win domestic political points. That's not responsible behaviour," Pawel Musialek of the Jagiellonian University suggests, in a country where politics is highly - and often viciously - polarised.

"So far we've seen no clear message that this guy was strongly ideological, and that it's not more like something from the darknet."

There has been no mention of any letter or manifesto that Oksinski left. One neighbour told Polish news site Onet that the 24-year-old was a "bit of a loner" but few who knew him well have spoken publicly.

What we know so far after Krakow gun attack

In addition to his electronic devices like his phone, investigators are examining a number of badges that Oksinski had attached to his military-style outfit.

The prosecutor's office says a yellow Gadsden flag with a coiled snake symbol was displayed in his bedroom. "It has various meanings of freedom and rebellion," a spokesman emphasised. "Not necessarily negative."

It was at Oksinski's home that the bodies of his parents and sister were discovered.

It is alleged that he shot them, before driving some 200km (125 miles) south to continue the killing in Krakow.

Candles and flowers have been placed around the square in places where someone died

Two days on, shops and cafes around the market square are beginning to fill up again, but the mood is subdued.

A Zara clothes shop is still boarded up, after bullets smashed the glass. A local man in his 50s died just outside.

Jo, a Scottish tourist, saw paramedics "who got there ridiculously fast", fighting to save him. "It was all pretty grim."

Like many, she initially mistook the rifle shots for fireworks.

On the opposite corner of the square, beside another cluster of flowers, people keep pausing to take pictures of the holes that were blasted into the wall by gunfire.

One of the six victims has been identified as a Polish painter in her 50s, who was a mother of four, and in town with friends for an exhibition opening.

A British citizen and an Austrian who died are yet to be named publicly.

Several drivers of the horse-drawn carriages that are usually poised to trot tourists around the square were seriously injured.

Olha, a Ukrainian citizen, moved to Krakow for safety when Russia launched its full-scale invasion

Olha, who works in a nearby chocolate shop says everyone is now nervous.

On Saturday, people rushed into her store to escape the gunman who was tearing around in his car, firing through the window.

Olha is Ukrainian, and moved to Krakow when Russia invaded. "We left our own country for safety. This is supposed to be a safe place!" she said.

"It's so sad, and so scary. Especially that he drove all the way here for two hours, planning this and thinking about it."

She regrets that the gunman did not survive: "So many are dead, so many lives have been ruined, and now there will be no justice."

Political differences were set aside between President Karol Nawrocki (left) and Prime Minister Donald Tusk (right) at the vigil on Monday

When Oksinski's car was searched, four firearms and "hundreds of rounds of ammunition" were found, all owned legally by the alleged killer and his father.

Both were members of the Swit Starachowice sports club, where there is a shooting range. A spokesman recalls Michal Oksinski as unremarkable: average competition results and normal behaviour.

He had been just three times this year, most recently in August with his father. The club spokesman is adamant that it does not train combat firing, only shooting from a fixed point.

Gun ownership in Poland has grown considerably since war broke out in Ukraine just across the border, but this is the first mass shooting anyone here can remember.

Inevitably, it has brought scrutiny of the checks over who qualifies to handle such weapons, including psychological tests, as Michal Oksinski passed all of them.

On Monday, the government announced an urgent review.

"Thank you Mr Policemen, get better soon"

At the spot where Oksinski's car was intercepted and he was shot by police there is another memorial, this one in honour of the officers still in hospital.

Some have brought flowers, but a girl called Bianka has left a card that reads: "Thank you Mr Policemen, get better soon."

A couple of steps away, there's a series of bullet holes in the glass.

"This man was a real hero, he didn't hesitate," Ania says, referring to officer Jakub Kulik who was shot in the chest. His condition remains serious. "We're here to help him fight for his life."

"Nothing like this has never happened before, it's terrible," a young man called Wojtek tells me.

He's only glad that the gunman responsible for such horror turned out to be Polish.

"People here are already against immigration, so that would have been even worse."

Ania thinks Poles will pull through, though the country's image as a low-crime oasis has been shattered.

Most of all, she's angry that Michal Oksinski died without being held to account for the devastation.

#107. Belgian plan for ICE-style immigration raids on homes criticised as ‘law of fear’

Rights groups oppose law that will let police forcibly enter homes to remove refused asylum seekers

Belgium could see ICE-style home raids that echo the US crackdown on immigration, rights campaigners have warned, after MPs approved a law that allows police to forcibly enter properties to remove undocumented people.

For months, intense debate has swirled across Belgium over measures introduced by the ruling right-leaning coalition to ramp up expulsions of people who have been denied the right to stay in the country.

In the run-up to the parliamentary vote last Friday, more than 60 protests were held across the country, adding to criticism voiced by NGOs, judges and lawyers’ associations over the measures. Some have drawn parallels with the US, where arrests by Immigration and Customs Enforcement have soared under Donald Trump’s administration.

The law, which is set to take effect next spring, gives the Belgian immigration office the power to ask an investigating judge to authorise entry into residences that may be housing anyone without papers, including homes where migrants are being hosted by friends or family.

Once this warrant has been obtained, police will be able to enter people’s homes without consent between 5am and 9pm, potentially in the presence of children. Once inside, they can detain people and carry out searches for identity documents.

“It’s a law of fear,” said Carine Thibaut, the director of Amnesty International’s French-speaking Belgian section, citing the impact the raids could have on children and on people who had opened their homes to migrants. “It really disseminates fear.”

Officials have said the measures will be targeted at people who have been denied asylum in Belgium, who have ignored orders to leave and who are seen as a threat to public order or national security.

A broad range of critics have said that the law is vague on what constitutes a public threat, citing cases where these terms were used for people who had carried out undeclared work, run a red light or shoplifted, meaning it will be up to individual judges to determine whether private homes can be turned into sites of immigration enforcement.

According to Amnesty International, the judges would not have full investigative powers but instead would be limited on who they could ask for additional information, while the legislation fails to provide a means for people to appeal or challenge any decisions that are made.

“They always say it’s for criminals,” Thibaut said. “We always answer that they already have all the tools to address this problem, because they can do that through the normal justice system.”

Her organisation is among the 20 rights groups that have warned that the law perpetuates the conflation of lack of legal status with delinquency. “The home is inviolable. This constitutional principle should never depend on the administrative status of those who live there,” they said.

The view was echoed at the Brussels-based Platform for International Cooperation on Undocumented Migrants, known as Picum. “This really has nothing to do with people who have committed crimes,” said Chiara Catelli, an advocacy officer. “It’s really about wanting to return people, deporting people, which is an administrative procedure.”

She added: “We’ve all seen the devastating consequences of home raids in the US, where they fuel fear and violence and tear families apart. It’s deeply alarming that an EU member state is now moving in the same direction.”

Catelli noted that similar, hardline measures were already in place in countries such as the Netherlands and Germany. The EU’s recently approved returns regulation, aimed at ramping up deportations across the bloc, includes similar provisions, meaning other member states could soon adopt comparable measures, she said. “We’re really afraid that this is the beginning of something that will become more widespread.”

Thibaut said that measures such as those in Belgium would gradually chip away at the rights of everyone. “It’s the beginning of reducing the state of law and the respect for human rights,” she said.

“This time it is for people without papers; after that, it might be people who are using drugs. That’s the way you slowly, slowly dismantle a society based on human rights, on respect for justice and on separation of powers”.

#106. Inside devastated front-line city that Russia wants Ukraine to give up

Meet the Peaky Blinders, Ukraine's drone unit protecting devastated front-line city

The dangers in Slovyansk are clear from the start.

"If I say run, you run," Tetiana tells us. She and her colleague Yasha are escorting us into a city now under constant bombardment from Russian drones, missiles and glide bombs.

It is raining and cloudy as we enter the city, and our military escorts call this "good" weather, meaning it is bad for drones.

But the threat is still there, and Yasha keeps his eyes peeled on the skies, while 21-year-old Tetiana scours her phone for the latest military intelligence on likely threats from the air.

They are members of a drone unit who call themselves Peaky Blinders and their distinctive emblem is a flat cap of the type made popular by the cult BBC TV series, although theirs are camouflaged.

Slovyansk is one of four cities that make up Ukraine's "fortress belt" - a system of heavily reinforced defence lines in the parts of eastern region of Donetsk still under its control.

Russia wants Kyiv to give up the fortress cities and they are central to talks on a possible peace deal that have resumed after nine months between Ukraine, Russia and the US in Abu Dhabi.

Ukrainians worry that doing so would make it much easier for Russia to advance further west, including to the capital Kyiv.

Very little of the city has been left unscathed by months of heavy bombardment

Once inside the city, we leave our vehicles hidden under the cover of trees and venture out on foot. Yasha carries a shotgun â a last resort to shoot down Russian drones. In halting English, Tetiana tells us walking is safer than travelling by car.

Abandoned vehicles litter the streets â recently destroyed by remotely operated Russian first-person view drones. The anti-drone netting that covers the roads is no guarantee of safety.

Tetiana shows us a video on her phone taken a few days earlier.

You can hear several drones buzzing above her, and then the cacophony of small arms fire as Ukrainian forces try to shoot them down. Tetiana admits it can be scary but explains how she felt compelled to help her country, abandoning her dreams of becoming an opera singer.

Slovyansk is now a husk of its former self. Very few buildings have been left unscathed, with many reduced to rubble.

Tetiana shows us a school recently destroyed by Russian glide bombs. Across the road there is a gaping hole in an abandoned residential block. Broken glass litters the pavement. The few shops still functioning are boarded up â making it hard to distinguish what is still open. Most have closed.

Tetiana says the Russians are doing everything they can to intimidate people and drive them out of the city. That, she says, "is their strategy â to make people leave in chaos, gathering up their last belongings and fleeing amid enormous fear and suffering. They want people to have nowhere left to live".

It is a familiar pattern already seen in other Ukrainian towns and cities in the Donbas â like Pokrovsk and Bakhmut. These are now ghost towns reduced to rubble, but eventually falling into Russian hands.

Russia has already taken nearly 90% of the Donbas â made up of the regions of Luhansk and Donetsk - but its advances this year have all but stalled in the face of strong Ukrainian resistance.

The White House is sounding positive about the revived talks in Abu Dhabi, despite Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky's refusal to cede the territory.

October was the deadliest month for Russian troops since the start of its full-scale invasion â with more than 54,000 killed or injured, according to Ukraine's general staff. Despite this, Russian forces are still making life in the Fortress Belt cities almost impossible.

Iryna, who works in one of the city's last remaining cafes, is one of the few residents who decided to stay

Before the war, Slovyansk was a city of more than 200,000 people. Less than 10% of those remain and the population diminishes by the day.

But amid the wail of near constant air raid warnings, there are still signs of people clinging on to life here. Iryna works in one of the city's last remaining coffee shops. Slovyansk is where she was born.

"I'm forced to walk around crying," she tells me. "How can they do this?"

Life, she says, has become incredibly difficult. She has already lost her house. But despite her tears, there is still defiance: "I won't leave Slovyansk. This is my home."

Inga, 53, was also born and raised here and continues to work in the city despite the danger.

"It's stressful. Honestly, I don't even know what to say," she says, but she is staying because of what she calls the "fear of the unknown".

"I simply have nowhere to go, and I don't have the money to leave or support myself afterwards." Nor, she says, is there anywhere else in Ukraine she would consider truly safe.

Commander Oleksandr of the Peaky Blinders special forces unit says neither side is able to advance because of the large number of drones in use

We first met the Omega National Guard Special Forces drone unit two years ago fighting in the Kharkiv region, in north-eastern Ukraine.

Just four of the original team remain - the rest have moved to other units, or they have been wounded or killed.

Among the dead was the brother of the man in charge of the unit, Oleksandr, who now describes his unit as his family.

He jokes it is just like the Peaky Blinders TV show â "but without the crime and the drugs". Back at their base they share a meal every evening.

Oleksandr reads out a prayer of thanks for surviving another day â he says it strengthens their spirits. The meal is accompanied by classical music played on a speaker. It helps drown out the noise of the explosions nearby.

"I feel this is the most difficult situation we've ever faced," says Oleksandr, who underlines the importance of holding the line.

"We must be here to inflict maximum damage on the enemy. We're fighting here because this is where the most intense phase of the war is taking place. Russia's claim that they will reach the borders of the Donbas, seize it, and then the war will end is absolutely false," he insists.

The remaining fortress belt cities provide Ukraine with a strong defensive line and are a major obstacle to any Russian advance. The saturation of drones in the area is also now making it nigh-on impossible for either side to advance here.

Peace talks may have resumed, but Oleksandr notes they have not led to anything so far.

"I really want this war to end," he says, "but do I believe these negotiations will succeed? No, I don't really believe they will".

The streets are largely empty, with increasing numbers of residents evacuated as drone attacks intensify

The next morning we are not the only ones making an exit. A dozen civilians have arrived at an evacuation point in the centre to be escorted out.

They hold all the belongings they can carry and are taken into a bunker for their safety. Outside there is another heavy blast of small arms fire, signalling more drones are nearby.

Alina Shyrko, herself a resident of Slovyansk, is co-ordinating the move of civilians out of the city and says the situation has deteriorated rapidly over the past few months. Families with children have already been ordered to leave.

"Everyone is thinking about leaving," she says, "I'm thinking about it too."

Ukraine still holds Slovyansk but its lifeblood, its people, are ebbing away.

Additional reporting by Rebecca Hartmann, Volodymyr Lozhko and Moose Campbell.

#105. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#101. 'They will kill you too': Sonia Gandhi's turbulent journey in Indian politics

Sonia Gandhi has been one of the most powerful political figures in India's history

On the morning of 31 October 1984, Sonia Gandhi was a young wife and mother, living in the prime minister's residence in Delhi. She was about to take a bath when she heard shots ring out in the garden.

Her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, India's prime minister, had just left for her office in an adjoining building. Moments later, she would be assassinated by two of her Sikh bodyguards, in retaliation for the Indian army's storming of the Golden Temple in Amritsar, Sikhism's holiest shrine, earlier that year.

The sound was like firecrackers, but "different". The children's nanny came pounding on the bathroom door, hysterical, pointing towards the garden.

Sonia ran towards the path. A white Ambassador car was moving slowly towards her. Inside, Indira lay covered in blood, her sari stained, her eyes open, her face wearing what Sonia remembered as a look of surprise.

"Mummy, Mummy," she tried to say. No words came. At the hospital, she waited for hours before a doctor's shake of the head told her what she feared.

The ordeal did not end there. Rajiv Gandhi, her husband and the elder son of Indira Gandhi, summoned from a rally in West Bengal, arrived to tell her that party leaders wanted him to become prime minister.

Sonia's response was fury and terror. "They will kill you; they will kill you, too!" she pleaded. Rajiv replied: "They will kill me anyway." That evening, as Indira's body lay at home, Rajiv was sworn in.

It is one of the most searing passages in Belonging: A Journey of Love, Sonia Gandhi's new memoir: private devastation unfolding alongside the pressures of public life.

The woman who had largely stayed away from politics was now married to India's prime minister, and would eventually find herself at the head of the Congress party, leading it through two general election victories and becoming one of the country's most consequential political figures.

The passage also foreshadows another devastating loss. In 1991, Rajiv himself was assassinated.

Indira Gandhi was asssassinated by her Sikh bodyguards in October 1984

For Sonia, the years that followed were marked by retreat.

At 19, a young woman from a small Italian town, she had met Rajiv, an Indian student, in England. Their relationship took her into a new country and family, and a close bond with Indira followed. India became home through her husband and her mother-in-law.

After Rajiv's killing, even the public appearances required by the memorial trusts she headed became an ordeal. Before speeches, she feared tears would overwhelm her.

When the Dalai Lama asked whether she felt vengeful towards her husband's killers, she answered, "No, I don't," and burst into sobs. Slowly, she writes, Rajiv became "a presence within me rather than a piercing absence".

But grief returned at family milestones. At her son Rahul's Cambridge graduation in 1995, she and her children were overcome by the thought of how proud Rajiv would have been.

Politics, meanwhile, was closing in. The Congress party, the political vehicle of her husband's family for generations, was losing cohesion and electoral ground. Senior leaders were defecting, and the calls for Sonia to step in were growing louder.

She initially refused. She writes she had seen politics through Rajiv: its achievements, but also its "ambition and greed, hypocrisy and treachery". "I'd always disliked politics," she writes. It had taken first her mother-in-law, then "the love of my life, my children's father".

Yet the party's decline began to feel personal. Walking past photographs of Indira and Rajiv, she was seized by "a mixture of pain and guilt". She feared that the forces they had fought to "protect India's pluralistic ethos" were gaining ground: a rising Hindu nationalism that, in her view, threatened the "secular and pluralistic vision" of India they had sought to defend.

Congress leaders argued that without her, the party could splinter, leaving little to resist communal politics. The family name, she believed, carried an appeal beyond religion, caste, language and region. This, she says, was "the most compelling argument".

Rajiv Gandhi took oath as India's youngest prime minister hours after his mother's death

Her doubts were also about her own credentials. How could an outsider occupy a position held by two formidable figures? At home, Rahul and Priyanka feared losing their mother as they had lost their grandmother and father. Before a New Year holiday, they extracted a promise: "Don't you do anything crazy while we're away."

The "answer", she says, came through the Bhagavad Gita. Though not religious in adult life, Sonia arranged private lessons with a Sanskrit scholar in January 1997. They read the text verse by verse. She writes that its central message â to perform one's dharma, or duty, without attachment to the fruits of one's actions â eventually resolved her inner conflict.

"I had the answer to my own doubts and questions. I had to step out and go where my duty called. And I did."

The memoir's most dramatic political scene, however, comes seven years later, after Congress's 2004 election victory. Sonia had led the party's campaign.

Sonia goes with Manmohan Singh to meet India's president at the presidential palace in Delhi and formally stake the Congress-led alliance's claim to form a government. Everyone assumes she will become prime minister. She says nothing.

At home, she tells a senior aide she intends to refuse. Party workers gather at the gates; senior colleagues demand a meeting.

"You fought the election, you led us," says one. "People believed in you. How can you let them down like this? Our workers will revolt. You think they'll accept another leader?"

Alliance leaders arrive to persuade her. They surround her, she recalls, and one insists: "We will not take no for an answer." Overwhelmed, she murmurs "Theek hai" - all right. They take it as consent and begin congratulating her.

Her children are appalled, she writes. Rahul storms in: "You said yes?" Her daughter Priyanka urges her not to be forced into a decision.

Sonia writes she spends a long night arguing with herself, friends and family. The risk is not merely personal. She believes the main opposition Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) would continue to exploit her foreign birth and that accepting office would allow the controversy to "create havoc" and hurt the country. By morning she is clear. "I've made up my mind. I'm going to stick to my stand," she tells Rahul.

Sonia Gandhi (centre), with daughter Priyanka and son Rahul, at the funeral of Rajiv Gandhi in 1991

She chooses Singh, a trained economist and a party member, instead, describing him as a man "above reproach", with a "deep and consummate understanding of governance".

The book is at its most revealing when it turns from family memory to the India she believes has been lost.

Sonia says that when she entered public life there was "a certain decency and decorum in parliamentary politics".

Political attacks and harsh words were not new, but she sees a sharper appetite among those in power "to belittle and torment those who differ from them, to intimidate those they perceive as opponents". It is, she says, part of a wider international drift towards "a heartlessness that accompanies authoritarian regimes".

Her portrait of prime minister Narendra Modi of the Hindu nationalist BJP is unusually direct.

The two have had "practically no real contact", she writes, beyond formal greetings at parliamentary gatherings and occasional exchanges - "Theek hain, aap?" ("Are you all right?") - when she returned from hospital. "He tends to look away," she says, "avoiding eye contact."

Sonia Gandhi with Manmohan Singh, whom she chose as prime minister after declining the post herself when Congress returned to power in 2004

Modi's idea of India, she writes, was shaped by the Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh (RSS) and "affirmed a fundamentalism in which his faith and culture superseded all others". His is an India of "hierarchy, order, and control"; hers is "an India against hegemony", standing for "inclusiveness, secularism, and social justice".

There is a striking concession amid the polemic - she says she understood Modi's "discomfort with the ruling elite of our times".

Sonia says she felt uneasy in Delhi's privileged drawing rooms, where political conversation seemed "very far removed from the reality of India".

Modi's origins, she writes, "may not be as humble as his PR team" suggests, but he "had certainly worked his way to power within the RSS from an underprivileged world". It is a rare acknowledgement of the social distance between the Congress establishment and the electorate Modi came to represent.

The memoir also offers some glimpses of Sonia's encounters with world leaders.

She remembers Vladimir Putin as an "engaging personality" who took her on a boat ride in St Petersburg, pointing out monuments along the way. She says she was impressed by his grasp of geopolitics and his "determination to restore Russia to its former powerful status" - "long before the Ukraine war, a different era".

Sonia Gandhi writes that she and PM Modi (foreground) have had "practically no real contact"

A more amusing encounter involved France's Nicolas Sarkozy, who arrived in India in 2008 without his then partner Carla Bruni because they were unmarried.

At a state banquet, the Indian vice-president's wife cheerfully grilled him about when he planned to marry her, whether he missed her and whether a date had been fixed. Sarkozy obligingly answered her questions while messaging Bruni on his phone.

Belonging is not a neutral history. The Bofors gun scandal, which badly damaged Rajiv Gandhi's government and dogged the family for decades, gets a relatively brief treatment.

The 502-page memoir is less forthcoming about the less flattering aspects of Congress's own record. Sonia has much to say about political corruption, hypocrisy and the decline of public standards under her opponents, but largely sidesteps the culture of patronage, cronyism and corruption that dogged Congress during its years in power. There is little reckoning, either, with the party's own share of responsibility for the erosion of public trust.

She is at pains to defend the political inheritance of her husband and mother-in-law, even as the party they built has suffered a prolonged decline. The story she tells is as much about the costs of carrying that inheritance as it is about a woman who says she never wanted the life she ended up leading.

That gives the memoir's final pages an unmistakable melancholy. Sonia, 79, is now a member of the Rajya Sabha, the upper house of parliament. Rahul, 55, is a four-term MP and leader of the opposition; Priyanka, 53, is also a member of parliament.

Sonia says the challenge was to remain faithful to "a just and humane society", through pain and loss, victory and defeat, and the manoeuvring that politics demands. But she ends not with triumph, nor even with the assurance that history will vindicate her.

"At this moment it seems to me that, although I tried my best, I didn't succeed in beating back the forces I'd set out to fight."

What will Sonia Gandhi's memoir reveal about India's most powerful dynasty?

#100. Nissan reduces petrol car production as EV sales boom in China

Manufacturer says ‘shift to new energy vehicles is happening faster than expected’ and market is harsh

The Japanese carmaker Nissan has said it has been caught out by accelerating sales of electric and hybrid cars in China amid a brutal price war in the world’s biggest automotive market.

The manufacturer slashed its sales forecasts for China and said it would cut back on petrol car manufacturing while increasing production of battery electric vehicles and plug-in hybrids, which combine a smaller battery with a petrol engine.

Nissan said it expected to sell between 2.97m and 3m cars this year, down from a previous forecast of 3.15m, mainly because of struggles in China.

“China remains our biggest challenge,” said Ivan Espinosa, Nissan’s chief executive. “Demand is weaker, competition is intensifying, and the shift to new energy vehicles is happening faster than expected. The market is being very, very harsh.”

The company reported increased sales in its home market as well as in North America, regions in which the shift to electric cars has been much slower because of policy decisions, including Donald Trump’s tearing up of incentives for battery car makers.

That divergence between different parts of the world illustrates the challenge for global carmakers, many of whom are trying to continue selling their more profitable petrol and diesel cars for as long as possible while risking being left behind by newer Chinese rivals.

China’s government gave weighty subsidies to its electric car industry, spotting an opportunity to overtake other countries such as Germany, Japan and the US, which had dominated sales of internal combustion engine cars.

That led to a huge number of Chinese EV competitors, which are now chasing a shrinking market. Nissan said sales across China fell by 22% in the first half of the year. That pressure “intensified from July through October” and left the carmaker’s sales down 32% in the first nine months of the year, Nissan said.

Espinosa said Nissan’s product lineup in China, led by the Sylphy family of saloon cars, was too heavily associated with petrol engines, so the company was cutting local production, exporting some products, while trying to ramp up its N series of models, which are electric or plug-in hybrids.

Yet at the same time as pushing for increased electric car production in China, Nissan has been among the manufacturers slowing the shift to batteries in Europe. In the UK the carmaker has lobbied to weaken the zero-emission vehicle mandate, a target for increasing EV sales, and to continue to sell petrol cars after 2035.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

That lobbying came despite evidence of rising demand for electric cars in the UK, particularly since the Iran war pushed up petrol and diesel prices. Data this week showed that sales of used electric vehicles rose much faster than petrol cars.

Ian Plummer, the chief customer officer at the car sales website Autotrader, said: “The standout trend in the used car market is the rapid rise of retail sales of electric vehicles, up an eye-popping 56% year on year in October and now accounting for one in 10 retail sales on Autotrader.”

Nissan’s only European factory is in Sunderland in northern England. Espinosa said he was hopeful that the company’s products from Sunderland and Japan would be included in proposed “Made in Europe” rules from the EU. The rules as drafted would exclude all of Nissan’s cars from subsidies and other incentives within the EU, adding to pressure on the carmaker.

#99. Why does Australia want Brits to swap Sydney for the outback?

Paige Tomlinson from Barnsley loves the "chilled" lifestyle of Cairns in northern Australia

In a Mexican restaurant in north-east Australia, the tacos are served by staff from France, Italy, Ireland and Argentina.

Cairns has long attracted a young and transient international crowd, who enjoy the tropical lifestyle while earning money for travel.

But one nationality is in shorter supply than it used to be.

"You get far fewer British backpackers looking for jobs here these days," says Mexicairns owner Peter McLeay.

It's a trend McLeay has noticed since mid-2023, when a free trade deal between the UK and Australia kicked in.

That agreement - as well as tariff-free trade in Tim Tams and Penguins - included special treatment for Brits on working holiday visas: exemption from having to find jobs in regional or remote places if they wanted to remain beyond a year.

That, along with an increase in the age limit from 30 to 35 saw backpacker visas issued to Brits surging from 47,200 in 2023-24 to just over 77,000 in 2025-26, according to the latest Australian government data.

Without the incentive to go regional, many are choosing to base themselves in the big cities, or, according to Prime Minister Anthony Albanese, "Bondi or Coogee" - two of Sydney's most famous beachside suburbs.

Now though, with Brits making up almost a quarter of all 323,000 backpacker visas issued last year, his government wants the free trade deal to be revisited.

Many British backpackers end up in Bondi, PM Anthony Albanese says, instead of exploring the rest of Australia

"Many Brits don't treat it as a working holiday anymore. It's more a three-year career break," says Dan McConnell, general manager of the Mad Monkey backpacker hostel chain.

He says Brits used to be the largest clientele at its Cairns accommodation but "now French outnumber British three to one".

"We see the Brits get to Sydney, decide it's the coolest place in the world, and stay there," he adds.

It's a privilege not enjoyed by working holidaymakers from other countries who must do at least 88 days of "specified regional work" in industries that need a seasonal or temporary workforce - including agriculture, fisheries, tourism and hospitality - if they want to qualify for a second year extension, and six months to stay a third year.

Barely a month ago, Albanese ruled out forcing British backpackers to do the same, accepting it as part of the trade deal.

But, under pressure from businesses here in Far North Queensland and other regional Australian communities, who are concerned about a lack of labour, the mood music has changed.

Last week Albanese met UK counterpart Andy Burnham at the meeting of Commonwealth leaders in the Caribbean.

And alongside AI, gender violence online and the AUKUS defence pact, backpackers were on the agenda.

Despite speculation in the Australian media, no immediate announcement was made. Albanese instead told reporters that he had pushed for a change in the rules on backpackers and that Burnham had been "very constructive about it".

"We'll work those issues through over coming weeks," he said adding that the free trade agreement was not set in stone and tweaks could be made "if there are good policy reasons".

Backpackers make up one in seven farm jobs across Australia, with about 52,000 people on working holiday visas doing jobs in horticulture each year, according to the National Farmers Federation.

It welcomed Albanese's discussions with Burnham, but said the issue of worker shortages in agriculture was far broader than the UK deal.

Of greater concern are wider proposals, announced in September, to cut net migration into Australia, under political pressure from the resurgent right wing populist One Nation party and the opposition coalition.

That includes an overhaul of the working holiday visa system, introducing a ballot for backpackers wanting to extend their stay â capped at 45,000 for people wanting a second year in Australia (down from 57,000 who qualified this year) and just 5,000 allowed to remain for a third year (compared with 31,000 this year).

This, the federation says, is "a low blow".

Those plans are still out for consultation, but immigration officials have slowed processing rates for some working holiday visa applications and paused them from several countries.

Tourism Tropical North Queensland chief executive Mark Olsen has also been among those lobbying Albanese in Canberra - telling him that backpackers brought both the spending power and the temporary labour needed to fill seasonal roles in a region famed as a gateway to the Great Barrier Reef and the Unesco world heritage listed Daintree rainforest.

Young Brits temporarily living and working in this region peaked at around 20,000 he says. Now it's closer to 11,000.

"Numbers have dropped significantly since the UK free trade agreement kicked in when border arrangements returned to normal [after the pandemic] in 2023 ," he said.

Peter Inderbitzen (right) relies heavily on seasonal workers including backpackers at his farm in Queensland

Cairns restaurateur Fintan Rafferty says that of about 180 staff on his payroll across four venues, only five are British. Prior to the visa rule change, that was closer to 25.

UK workers came with good skills in hospitality and "were willing to work, and to work flexible hours", he told the BBC.

And, he said, it was a two-way street. "The pay is very good, the conditions are very good. And one of the great things about the 88-days rule is that it forces people to travel. It's enriching their experience."

Bucking the trend, at the bar of Gilligan's backpackers, Paige Tomlinson serves vodka and soda with plenty of ice and a broad South Yorkshire accent.

The 27-year-old, from Barnsley, has been in Cairns for four months, attracted by the "chilled" lifestyle and local waterfalls.

"Most people prefer Sydney and they question me all the time why I'm here," she laughs. "But I got to Sydney and I just didn't like it. It was too busy, too much like London and I don't like London."

She says she'd like to see the regional work requirement brought in for all Brits, "getting them out their comfort zone".

"If every other country's got to do it, why should we not?"

About 200km north, at Red Valley Farm in Lakeland, general manger Peter Inderbitzen agrees.

Workers mainly from Tonga, hired on a Pacific labour scheme, pick bananas there year-round.

But Inderbitzen also relies heavily on backpackers, especially at peak avocado season, employing about 25 people for a couple of months each year.

"It's very short, sharp, get it done, get it out kind of work," he told the BBC. "But without those pickers, the avos stay on the tree."

Ethan Phillips, 23, from Buckinghamshire says the new Australian proposals make sense

While a few British backpackers were still applying for work, numbers had "completely dropped off" since the rule change, said Inderbitzen, who'd "love" to see British travellers having an incentive to head to the regions.

"As much as it wasn't perfect, [that programme] was working."

Back in Cairns, most of the young Brits the BBC tracks down are on tourist visas - but many want to return for a working holiday.

Among them is Ethan Phillips, 23, from Buckinghamshire who has been having way too much fun to keep up to date with what politicians have been discussing.

"It makes sense for them to want us to do [regional work] again," he says. "I mean I'd rather not do it, but if we have to, it's only three months â¦ it can't be that bad."

#95. US envoys to meet Putin and Zelenskyy separately after peace talks see some progress - Europe Live

US negotiators to meet Putin in Moscow, Zelenskyy in Dublin next

Russia’s chief negotiator ⁠at Ukraine peace talks in Abu Dhabi Vladimir ⁠Medinsky said ​after their conclusion that mutual understanding ⁠had been achieved on some issues and that more ⁠talks would be held.

Medinsky, ​in ‌remarks broadcast ‌on Russian state television, said that ‌Russia and Ukraine had agreed on a large-scale prisoner swap, the latest in a long line of ‌such exchanges.

He was cool however on the ​prospect of European countries joining the talks and said it was unclear what ⁠there was for Russia and the ​European ​Union to ​discuss, given what he ​said ‌was a lack ​of ​proposals from the European side, Reuters reported.

Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy also picked up on calls to include Europe in any future peace talks (12:44), saying “Europe must be represented at every stage of real diplomacy.”

In a post on X after getting a briefing on the Abu Dhabi talks, he said:

“First, there is an important humanitarian arrangement regarding preparations for a new exchange.

Second, the parties discussed their views on guaranteeing security, and it is necessary that the first step be ending this war and providing security guarantees for Ukraine.

Third, broader formats for further work involving the Europeans were discussed.

Next, the American representatives are heading to Russia, and after those talks, we will coordinate our positions once again and determine what next steps are possible. We are meeting with them tomorrow in Dublin.

Ukraine’s priority is to end the war as soon as possible with a dignified peace. We believe that Europe must be represented at every stage of real diplomacy.

It is essential that the United States have a strong position and exert influence on the source of this war – that is Russia – so that Russia ends the war of its own making.”

Meanwhile, a senior Russian diplomat outlined a series of proposals that could form a part of any future security guarantees for Russia, Reuters reported.

Speaking at a security conference in Moscow, Alexey Drobinin set out three scenarios.

The first option was that of a collective defensive alliance or of bilateral mutual assistance agreements like those Russia has with Belarus ‌and North Korea.

The second possibility he cited included political agreements not to help each other’s adversaries in ​the event of aggression, like the pact Moscow has with Iran.

And the third option he presented were bilateral mutual non-aggression pacts.

“For example, agreements of this kind could be reached, among other things, with individual ⁠Nato member states, which would then receive, for instance, additional ​individual security guarantees ​from the Russian Federation,” ​Drobinin told the conference, according to video of his ​speech posted online.

The G7 top diplomats stressed the need to secure long-term financial aid for Ukraine, as Kyiv’s allies sought ways to cover its budget beyond 2026, AFP reported.

“We acknowledged the need to address Ukraine’s pressing financing needs for 2027 and beyond in a coordinated way,” said a joint statement issued by foreign ministers of Canada, Germany, France, Italy, Japan, the UK and United States, following a meeting held Monday and Tuesday in Paris.

“We reiterated that negotiations on the future security in Europe must include the Europeans,” it added, as US-mediated talks between Russia and Ukraine took place in Abu Dhabi.

Meanwhile, just a quick update on Italy: the country’s prime minister Giorgia Meloni said the email bomb alerts sent to school (11:36) pose no danger, but police and other public bodies were monitoring the situation.

“These reports appear to be part of a strategy intended to cause alarm and concern – a matter that is currently under thorough investigation,” she said.

Meloni will chair a meeting on the situation later today to assess what may need to be done after “hundreds” of schools saw their plans for today disrupted by the alarm.

A large number of schools in Northern Ireland were also ​sent a threatening email, the Northern Ireland ‌Education Authority said, via Reuters, but have been advised to stay open as the authority said police did not regard it as ‌a credible threat.

Kushner also said he thought “it would be great” for Zelenskyy and Putin to meet in person to hold further talks, but acknowledged “the Russians have said they would like to see more progress made before that happens.”

“Clearly, there’s not great trust or love between the leaders, so I think that if we can find a pathway to arrange…I will say this, … these deals do take time.”

Kushner also acknowledged that there is “no trust between the sides and that’s to be expected after a conflict like this,” but he hoped he could make progress on that.

He said it is important to have Europe at the table for any discussions on Ukraine and postwar security as “it’s a discussion where they have to be involved,” and pointedly praised his engagement with top Europeans so far.

He said he hoped Putin would “give his blessing” to expanding the format during their meeting in Moscow.

Speaking after today’s talks, US negotiator (and Donald Trump’s son-in-law) Jared Kushner described the discussions as “very substantive,” even if “pretty tough … after almost eight months of not being together.”

He said he and peace envoy Steve Witkoff would meet Putin in Moscow on Thursday to “go over what was discussed today and try to make progress on some of the issues that we discussed.”

Afterwards, they will fly to Dublin to meet with Ukraine’s Zelenskyy “to debrief with him and further explore some of the ideas that we came up with today,” he said.

Zelenskyy will be in Dublin at the European Political Community summit, alongside other EU leaders.

Kushner said he also hoped to “finalise the humanitarian relief” and “the biggest humanitarian release of prisoners” during tomorrow’s meeting with Putin.

He said the US was also working to “try and see if we can get the Europeans added to the format so that we can make this a multilateral format,” praising already existing discussions with European partners, including the EU, Nato, France, Germany, Italy, and the UK.

Kushner said the key issues revolve around “postwar security” and “security guarantees,” as well as “deconfliction” and monitoring any future ceasefire.

But he acknowledged that “the territorial issues … will be the hardest.”

Meanwhile, David Arakhamia, a close ally ‌of president Volodymyr Zelenskyy and ​the head of his faction ⁠in parliament, said he hoped to keep up “good momentum” in the talks with a view of reaching a solution by the end of the year.

Russia’s chief negotiator ⁠at Ukraine peace talks in Abu Dhabi Vladimir ⁠Medinsky said ​after their conclusion that mutual understanding ⁠had been achieved on some issues and that more ⁠talks would be held.

Medinsky, ​in ‌remarks broadcast ‌on Russian state television, said that ‌Russia and Ukraine had agreed on a large-scale prisoner swap, the latest in a long line of ‌such exchanges.

He was cool however on the ​prospect of European countries joining the talks and said it was unclear what ⁠there was for Russia and the ​European ​Union to ​discuss, given what he ​said ‌was a lack ​of ​proposals from the European side, Reuters reported.

Meanwhile, we are getting some lines from those present at the Abu Dhabi talks participating in the Ukrainian, Russian, and US delegations.

Zelenskyy also turns to Russia’s demands for security guarantees.

He points out that is not obvious given “no one has attacked Russia for many decades,” and yet “since the second world war, Russia has not gone through even one decade without starting a war or using military force against another country.”

“So when the Russians now talk about a security architecture for themselves, or about security guarantees for Russia, they are hardly talking about real security and peace.

What they really want is to freely interfere in the affairs of other countries, and to use force whenever they want, and to make sure that no one can support liberty inside Russia, because Putin wants to keep his personal power forever and pass on his imperialist and in many ways, simply fascist views to the next generations.”

He also says clearly that Europe “must be at the table” of any talks on ending the war in Ukraine, warning that “whenever Europe does not believe in its own strength, others try to impose their decisions on us.”

He gets warm applause and a standing ovation at the end of his speech.

Zelenskyy also talks about the need for Europe to develop “its own anti-ballistic defence programme,” Freyja to become independent of deliveries from overseas (mostly the US).

He also mentions the importance of drone technologies and learning from Ukraine’s real-life experience.

Zelenskyy brings up the Abu Dhabi discussions, but in quite general terms.

“Yesterday and today our Ukrainian team together with American team has been talking to the Russians in Abu Dhabi.

Putin started this war thinking he could break Ukraine in just days or few weeks. But here we are in the fifth year of the full scale war. The Russians have to sit across from our representatives and have to listen to Ukraine and to talk to us as equals about what comes next. They wanted to decide Ukraine’s future without us, without Ukrainian people, but they failed.”

Zelenskyy says the strait of Hormuz crisis creates a dangerous precedent, asking “what if Russia tries to do the same in Baltic Sea”?

“What if someone tries to repeat what happened in the Strait of Hormuz, this time in other major shipping routes and straits around the world? And who will protect European interests?”

He says the Iran crisis also has an effect on Ukraine, as is experiencing “the shortages of weapons we need for our defence.”

“Today, everyone in the world can see just how connected everything is in our world. Nothing stays local. Nothing. Especially wars and injustice,” he warns.

Zelenskyy then begins his speech proper, with a much more sombre point about the war in Ukraine.

He says that on 11 November, “people in many European countries remember those who lost their lives in war on the anniversary of the end of the first world war.”

“This is not just some distant history, unfortunately. What is happening in the world today brings our thoughts, our feelings, our fears back to those times when Europe and the whole world were turned upside down.”

He says “Russia has fallen for the same illusion as back then more than a hundred years ago. The illusion that it could simply grab more land, a few more colonies, more power and influence at the cost of Ukrainians independence.”

He also points to the events in the Middle East as an additional destabilising element, warning against “rising energy prices” and “one of the worst cost of living crises.”

Zelenskyy also jokes that given the warm relations between Denmark and Ukraine, he regrets the two countries “do not share a border.”

“You have much better neighbours,” he says in a swipe at Russia.

“After all, we have Russia as our neighbour and having a neighbour like Denmark is something any country would dream of right now.”

He then lands another joke about the US and Donald Trump:

“Maybe when someone in America wanted Greenland, he didn’t even realise that what he really wanted was not the land, but to become a little more Danish. And who knows, maybe that was exactly what he needed.”

Zelenskyy begins with words of thanks to PM Frederiksen and to Denmark on behalf of the Ukrainian people.

He opens with an interesting US-themed dig, as he notes he is the first foreign leader to address the Folketinget.

“I‘ve heard that many leaders wanted to be here, to speak here. One of them was even the president of the United States.

That was back in 1997. In the end, the American president spoke to the Danish people in the different place.”

Zelenskyy is now getting ready to address the Danish parliament.

#92. 'It can kill us': Kenyans fear impact of El Niño with floods predicted

Agnes Shikoli says she fears either drowning, or being crushed by her house

"It flooded up to here in March," Agnes Shikoli says, pointing to a spot halfway up the 6ft [1.8m] corrugated iron wall of her home in Kibera, one of the largest informal settlements in Kenya.

Her tiny house lies just a couple of metres from the Ngong river, which runs through this densely populated area in the capital, Nairobi.

The scars of floods in the long rainy season earlier this year can still be seen - including a broken TV in the corner which was immersed in water.

Tucked out of sight is a photo of her sister Beatrice Khalayi, 58, who died when intense rains caused widespread destruction in Kibera. She was among more than 60 people across the country who lost their lives.

"Someone called me to tell me that she had died of electrocution after her house flooded," Shikoli recalls. "When I see a picture of her, I feel uneasy, that's why I keep it far from me, so I don't see it."

With Kenya's short rainy season now starting, Shikoli tells the BBC she is afraid - especially following warnings that the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon could bring a new round of flooding.

"I feel like it can kill us. These houses aren't made of stone," says the 62-year-old. "They can fall on us, or the water can drown us."

Kibera - and Kenya more widely - are on the front line of preparations for the potential impact of El NiÃ±o.

Forecasters have warned that this year could see one of the strongest on record, bringing extreme weather to many parts of the world.

In southern Kenya, that means the threat of rains even more intense than usual.

Outside Shikoli's house, groups of men and women wearing high-visibility vests are shovelling and raking garbage out of the riverbed and disposing it far from the water.

This programme called Climate Worx, under the office of President William Ruto, is meant to rehabilitate Nairobi's rivers. Such exercises have intensified since August when warnings increased about the possible scale of this year's El NiÃ±o.

Rubbish clogging Nairobi's drains and sewers was seen as a major factor in the deadly floods earlier this year

Similar work to unclog drainage systems is ongoing across Nairobi, a sprawling city built on hilly ground crossed by three major rivers.

But the workers have a huge task on their hands.

Blocked drains and inadequate sewage systems were widely blamed for the high number of deaths after the floods earlier in the year, as the excess rainfall had nowhere to escape.

The Nairobi County disaster management chief, Bramwell Simiyu tells the BBC that drainage systems have been "untouched for many years" and that removing the rubbish from them will save lives.

Floods disproportionately affect informal settlements in Nairobi, especially those near rivers, like Kibera and Mathare.

In most of these settlements, houses sit just a few metres from rivers. Some people, including Shikoli, live on the slopes of the river itself due to the constant expansion of the settlements. When flash floods occur, these houses are swept away, endangering the lives of their occupants.

Nairobi's drainage system has not been upgraded to deal with the rapid growth in the population and infrastructure over the decades

"The existing drainage system was developed 30 years ago to cater for a population of about 2.5 million residents but we're dealing with about seven million in the daytime and 5.5 million night-time," Simiyu says.

He adds that investment of up to $192m (Â£143m) is needed to upgrade the sewerage and drainage system entirely.

But there is little sign that either the cash-strapped national or city council will able to raise this kind of money.

The Nairobi River Basin Regeneration programme, a government programme aimed at restoring the city's rivers, found that settlements near rivers, and where there is weak enforcement of regulations, have increased flooding risks. It recommended a "buffer" to protect rivers and "humane relocation" of communities where necessary.

However, rising poverty has driven many people to live close to rivers.

"Our worst case scenario looking at low-income settlements is that up to about 100,000 people are at risk," Simiyu says. "Moderate projections is about 5,000 households or about 20,000 people may be at risk."

Anyone forced to leave their homes will be relocated to 167 designated holding grounds where a stockpile of relief food and supplies will be distributed, Simiyu says.

Nobody knows exactly when the rains will come - or how much rain there will be.

But Kenya's meteorological department warns that November is usually the peak rainfall month of the short rainy season, which normally lasts from October to December.

A regional forecast from the Intergovernmental Authority on Development (Igad) warned in September that there was "an 81% probability of a very strong El NiÃ±o during October-December 2026".

So far, the heavy rains predicted have not occurred but experts remain concerned.

In East Africa, this El NiÃ±o is being widely compared to a similar weather event in 1997-1998 which caused widespread flooding and destruction in Somalia and Kenya.

Weather forecaster Jemima Gacheru tells the BBC that is because temperature conditions in the Indian Ocean in the 1997 El NiÃ±o and those recorded now are similar. This interaction, she says, has been seen to result in very strong rainfall.

"For this season we have a very strong El NiÃ±o already in play in the Pacific and then we have a positive Indian Ocean dipole," Gacheru said. This is often called the "Indian NiÃ±o" because of its similarity to its Pacific equivalent and refers to the difference in sea-surface temperatures in opposite parts of the Indian Ocean.

"We expect to have some enhanced rainfall over several parts of the country."

The UN's migration body the International Organization for Migration (IOM) has warned that almost 900,000 people could be forced to leave their homes in Kenya this year, mostly because of the predicted floods.

Dozens of people died in floods in Kenya earlier this year

Kenya is not the only African country worried about the potential impact of El NiÃ±o.

In neighbouring Somalia, the country's disaster management agency has warned that 1.2 million people are at risk of displacement caused by extreme weather - including the risk of both floods and drought.

The 1998 floods killed 2,000 people in Somalia and forced a further 250,000 to flee their homes, according to the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO). Up to 80% of crops were destroyed in some parts of the country.

The United Nation's famine warning system IPC has warned of the increased risk of food insecurity in Somalia due to supply chain issues exacerbated by the impact of El NiÃ±o.

Elsewhere on the continent forecasters fear El NiÃ±o could bring extreme heat and prolonged periods without rain.

It could cause a crisis for farmers across parts of West Africa, including the semi-arid Sahel region south of the Sahara, as well as in southern parts of the continent, where the risk of crop failure is high.

There are fears El NiÃ±o's impact will worsen existing issues.

Communities like Kibera are already grappling with poor housing and rampant poverty, while parts of Somalia are in the middle of a devastating drought.

National governments in many countries are doing what they can to prepare but the extent of El NiÃ±o's impact is impossible to predict.

Back in Kibera, Shikoli and her family are preparing for the worst.

Council officials have told them they should leave their home on the riverbank until the rainy season has passed but Shikoli makes just $2 a day selling groundnuts and fruit from a small stall next to the river.

"I want to move," she says. "But I don't have the means.

"We just have to persevere and leave it to God."

#89. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#85. Labor won’t scrap ABC and SBS ombudsmen in favour of external body as suggested by antisemitism envoy

Exclusive: Jillian Segal suggested ‘transferring’ powers in updated report released a fortnight ago but government sources note media regulator already exists

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Albanese government won’t follow a suggestion by the antisemitism envoy to scrap the ABC and SBS ombudsmen and replace them with an external oversight body.

In a list of updated priorities released last month, Jillian Segal proposed an “external independent ombudsman” to monitor ABC and SBS coverage of the war in Gaza and to ensure publicly funded broadcasters were “held accountable to their own stated standards of accuracy and impartiality”.

“Many in the Jewish community remain concerned that coverage they perceive as one-directional may contribute to a negative – and even hostile – view by audiences of those who support Israel, or have sympathy for it,” Segal said in her updated plan to combat antisemitism.

Segal said the government should consider “transferring” responsibilities from the ABC and SBS ombudsmen to an “external independent ombudsman consistent with other sectors such as telecommunications and banking”.

The ABC said at the time its complaints process was “the most robust and transparent in the Australian media” and both broadcasters stated they were subject to external review by the Australian Communications and Media Authority (Acma).

This week, government sources noted that Acma already acted as an external independent regulator that could investigate complaints if they needed to be escalated beyond the ABC and SBS ombudsmen.

A spokesperson for the federal communications minister, Anika Wells, on Wednesday thanked Segal for her work and said there was no place for antisemitism in Australia.

It was an evil “that tears at the fabric of our country … [and] puts the peaceful, free and equal society we cherish at risk”, they said.

It is understood Labor could consider changes in response to recommendations from the royal commission into antisemitism that is scheduled to report in mid-December. The commission, headed by former high court judge Virginia Bell, was established after the antisemitic Bondi beach massacre in December 2025.

“The government will soon receive the final report from the Royal Commission into Antisemitism and Social Cohesion,” Wells’ spokesperson said on Wednesday.

“[It] will continue to work with the special envoy, the Jewish community and all Australians to combat antisemitism and strengthen our social cohesion.”

Segal’s latest suggestion regarding the ABC and SBS received a mixed response a fortnight ago.

Alex Ryvchin, the co-chief executive of the Executive Council of Australian Jewry, welcomed Segal’s suggestions, warning meaningful oversight of coverage by public broadcasters could only be achieved “through external accountability”.

But the progressive Jewish Council of Australia criticised the envoy’s new plan as “Trumpian”, arguing it threatened freedom of speech and democratic rights.

The Australian Palestine Advocacy Network criticised attempts to conflate legitimate criticism of Israel with antisemitism and stated: “Speaking out against Israel’s ongoing atrocities is the prerogative of anyone who believes in human dignity and human rights.”

Segal in October wrote that the “independence of public broadcasters must, of course, be protected”.

“That independence, however, does not – and should not – amount to a right to be free from external scrutiny and empirical review. Indeed, ensuring that the processes withstand fair scrutiny can only increase confidence.”

The ABC ombudsman has previously investigated public complaints related to coverage of the Middle East and the war in Gaza, including the Israeli president’s visit to Australia and a press club address by the Israeli ambassador.

ABC Alumni, which represents hundreds of former staff, has said that since October 2023 the ABC ombudsman has upheld four out of thousands of complaints of bias or inaccuracy relating to Middle East coverage.

Acma, “to which dissatisfied complainants can appeal, has upheld none”, the group said in a statement, adding it was “outraged at the continued insistence by Jillian Segal … that the ABC and SBS, Australia’s most trusted news outlets, are contributing to antisemitism”.

In her first report in mid-2025, Segal initially proposed the special envoy be given powers to monitor media organisations to encourage “accurate, fair and responsible reporting” to ensure impartiality and balance and to avoid “accepting false or distorted narratives”.

She then told the royal commission into antisemitism in mid-2026 she wanted a new “oversight” committee that could give ABC and SBS coverage a “tick” or “guidance”.

Segal told Senate estimates in late October she was concerned about “the tone [and] the frequency of reporting about Gaza from the ABC”.
