#80. David Jones to close half its stores, triggering mass job losses in ‘urgent restructure’

Australia’s oldest department store collapsed into voluntary administration last week after years of steep losses

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

David Jones is set to shutter half of its department store network, triggering mass job losses under a sweeping restructure, the appointed receivers said on Wednesday.

David Jones employs more than 6,000 staff across 38 stores.

The receivers FTI Consulting said in a statement that it would proceed with an “urgent restructure of the business”.

“As part of this restructure, the receivers have commenced an immediate consultation process for all staff associated with 19 stores that have been identified for closure,” FTI said.

“For the immediate term these stores will continue trading and while there is no final date for the closures, we anticipate they will cease trading by the end of the year.”

The closures comprise seven stores in New South Wales, three each in Victoria and Western Australia, two each in Queensland and South Australia, one in the ACT, and David Jones’s sole New Zealand store in Auckland.

Under a dual appointment, FTI has day-to-day control of operations, while administrators McGrathNicol seek a buyer or fresh injection of capital.

The upheaval follows years of steep losses after Australia’s oldest department store failed to carve out a profitable niche in a sector disrupted by online competitors and stand-alone upmarket stores.

The company collapsed into voluntary administration last week. The David Jones chief executive, Erica Berchtold, told suppliers the business could not continue operating as it was.

“Put simply, our store portfolio is no longer the right shape for the business, and this change is about creating a network that is financially sustainable for the future,” Berchtold said in correspondence viewed by Guardian Australia.

As David Jones’s primary secured lender, Hilco Global holds a decisive role in the retailer’s fate, having appointed receivers FTI to protect its financial interests.

Hilco will place its workers inside the stores marked for closure from Thursday.

“These representatives will have a greater presence in the stores that are closing to assist in accelerating sales and promotional activity at a store level,” Berchtold said in the correspondence.

The closures largely affect smaller format stores, sparing David Jones’s city flagships.

Longtime Melbourne resident Lana Burgess, who was on Wednesday at the small Malvern Central store slated for closure, told Guardian Australia that David Jones had become too expensive.

“Shopping in the last 20 years has changed. In the old days people used to go into town,” said Burgess.

“Now … everyone does their shopping online. I think it’s to do with that and maybe Malvern, the area, is ageing; as I get older I don’t need to buy as much.

“And the rents – these huge properties. Of course the online shops can do it much cheaper.”

In documents released late on Tuesday, David Jones’s administrators revealed they reviewed the private equity-owned department chain’s finances months before it fell into administration.

Some small suppliers are owed money dating back to stock sent in April, well before David Jones missed rent payments and fell into administration.

Its private equity owner, Anchorage Capital, has long sought a slimmed down version of David Jones, free from long-term leases on underperforming sites. It is expected to make a proposal to administrators to repurchase the 188-year-old chain.

David Jones’s main landlord, Westfield owner Scentre Group, said its shopping malls were “practically full” despite the closures and that it could bring other brands into its 17 centres hosting the department stores.

Many of the chain’s other landlords had been aware of its struggles and were readying to bring in new tenants once closure dates were announced.

Its management and receivers will on Thursday meet with the union, the Shop, Distributive and Allied Employees’ Association, which will push for affected employees to be moved to other locations or be given their full redundancy entitlements, which are at risk as the business struggles to pay debts.

“Today these workers are receiving this terrible news through no fault of their own,” Bernie Smith, the union’s national vice-president, said.

“Many members have given a large part of their working lives to David Jones and have served the company with loyalty and pride.”

#78. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#75. Youpla directors disqualified over collapsed funeral fund that targeted Aboriginal families

Three directors were fined a combined $90,000 in federal court judgment and disqualified from managing a corporation for between five and 10 years

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Three directors of a collapsed funeral fund that marketed insurance to Aboriginal families have been banned from managing corporations for at least five years and ordered to pay a combined $90,000 after being found to have breached their duties to clients.

On Wednesday, federal court justice Geoffrey Kennett ordered the former director of the insurance company Youpla Group, previously the Aboriginal Community Benefit Fund (ACBF), Jonathan Law, to pay the commonwealth $50,000 and disqualified him from managing corporations for six years.

Michael Wilson, also a director, was ordered to pay $20,000 and disqualified from managing corporations for 10 years, while director Geoffrey Clayton was ordered to pay $20,000 and disqualified from managing corporations for five years.

The trio were also ordered to pay a combined $17,000 towards plaintiff’s costs. Kennett found they had failed to perform their duties with the degree of “care and diligence” expected, failed to manage conflicts of interest, and caused a detriment to the ACBF members.

It followed a judgment late last month where the founder of the company, Ron Pattenden, was ordered to pay a quarter of a million dollars and barred from managing corporations for 15 years. Pattenden acknowledged he had breached directors’ duties in a claim settlement with financial watchdog, the Australian Securities and Investments Commission (ASIC).

During the same judgment, the former Youpla Group chief Bryn Jones was found by Kennett to have breached his directorial duties after a contested liability hearing in February. He denied having knowledge of the fund’s insurance premium increases. Jones’s penalty will be determined at a hearing at a later date.

Youpla sold junk funeral policies to Indigenous people across Australia for decades, including targeting communities where English was not the first language and communities with low literacy levels.

It collapsed in March 2022, leaving more than 13,000 Aboriginal people, some elderly and in palliative care, without the means to pay for funerals. Families had to resort to crowdfunding and some were forced to leave their loved ones’ bodies in the morgue while they raised the funds.

In August 2023, Asic commenced proceedings in federal court against the five former directors and officers for breaches of their duties, alleging governance failures and director misconduct that affected the First Nations people who were members of the funds.

It alleged the directors and officers maintained insurance arrangements with Crown Insurance, a Vanuatu-based company owned and controlled by Pattenden and another director, Law, and did not act in the best interest of the ACBF entities and members.

Breaches of directors’ duties can attract a maximum penalty of $200,000 for each breach that occurred between 2017 and March 2019.

Youpla targeted Indigenous people using marketing materials in the distinctive red, black and yellow colours of the Aboriginal flag, including stuffed toys and colouring books for children, turning up to community events and by conducting door-to-door sales.

It was investigated by various regulators over the years but it was not until 2018, when its conduct was exposed in the banking royal commission, that its licence to sell new products was withdrawn.

In 2021 Asic questioned its solvency, and the company’s directors finally pulled the plug.

The federal court handed Youpla a $1.2m fine in September 2023 for misrepresenting junk funeral policies, finding it had told plan holders they would receive a lump sum payment when in fact they would only be reimbursed for expenses they could prove had been incurred.

Youpla avoided paying millions more in fines handed down by the federal court in July 2025 because it was in liquidation.

A $97m federal government support package was announced in February 2024 to help thousands of mostly low-income Aboriginal families recover from financial loss.

The government’s payments to remaining victims will continue to be processed until 1 December this year. Eligible recipients have the choice of a funeral bond or a cash payment worth 60% of the value of their policy.

#70. Lending to property investors falls at record pace, as Australians fall behind on home loans

Share of CBA customers struggling to repay their mortgages has climbed to 12-year high

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

New lending to property investors fell at its fastest pace on record through the three months to September, as landlords adjust to higher taxes against a backdrop of plunging property prices and rising interest rates.

The latest figures come as the country’s biggest lender, the Commonwealth Bank, reported that the share of its customers struggling to repay their mortgages had climbed to a 12-year high.

As Jim Chalmers, the treasurer, was again forced to defend his budget reforms, CBA’s quarterly trading update showed the share of borrowers 90 days or more behind on their repayments lifted from 0.73% of total lending, to 0.8%.

The last time arrears rates were higher was in 2014, according to previous trading updates.

Nathan Zaia, a senior equity analyst at Morningstar, said rising arrears rates were happening across the banking sector, and that they “probably hadn’t peaked”.

“Looking at arrears and people falling behind on their home loans, the trend has definitely been picking up,” Zaia said.

But the recent increase was from a low base, he said.

“We’ve had a long period of uncharacteristically very low arrears rates, so it’s not unexpected that they are starting to drift higher.”

Nico de Lange, the director of financial institution ratings at S&P Global, said that despite rising arrears, “it doesn’t look like there is any significant stress coming through” for the banking sector as a whole.

“Obviously after the five rate interest rate increases this year, we are watching things pretty closely,” de Lange said.

The latest Australian Bureau of Statistics figures showed the value of new investor loans plunged by nearly a third in the September quarter – the biggest drop in official records stretching back to 2002.

That was nearly double the previous biggest quarterly fall, the ABS said.

Mish Tan, the head of finance statistics at the ABS, said the data “suggests that investor activity is being influenced by factors beyond increasing borrowing costs, including the potential impacts of tax changes and expectations of lower house prices”.

The Albanese government in the May budget announced changes to the tax on capital gains for investors, and banned negative gearing for new landlords buying an existing home.

As investor appetite for housing collapsed by more than 30%, new lending to owner-occupiers fell by a more sedate 5.3% as part of an overall quarterly drop of 15% in new loans.

The ABS data also showed lending to first home buyers dropped in the quarter by 8.6%, suggesting rising interest rates and worries about the trajectory of property prices were keeping would-be homeowners on the sidelines – if not to the same degree as investors.

Bu Chalmers said the ABS figures also showed falling competition for first-time buyers as investors fled the market.

“Our tax reforms are all about helping more first home buyers into the housing market and we expect to see those benefits play out over years, not months,” he said.

“Because of our reforms, first home buyers will have to compete less with established investors for limited existing housing stock, and we’re starting to see that in this data.”

The number of new loans to investors to build a home was down 12% in the quarter, the ABS figures showed.

The Coalition has promised to repeal the investor tax changes were it to win government.

Andrew Bragg, the shadow housing minister, said: “Labor’s higher housing taxes are discouraging investment when Australia desperately needs more homes”.

“It is time these taxes were repealed before they do any more damage,” Bragg said.

#69. Tearful Smith switches allegiance to Netherlands

Tatjana Smith won a gold and silver medal at each of the past two Olympics

South Africa's most decorated Olympian Tatjana Smith was in tears as she announced that she has switched allegiance to the Netherlands.

The 29-year-old won four Olympic breaststroke medals, including two golds, before retiring after the Paris 2024 Games.

She came out of retirement in September and hopes to compete in the 50m breaststroke as the event makes its Olympic debut at the 2028 Games in Los Angeles.

Smith, whose maiden name was Schoenmaker, wiped away tears as she read a letter addressed to South Africa, saying that "dreams can change as we grow".

Smith has dual citizenship because her father is Dutch.

She said she has "always been Dutch and South African" and that this is an opportunity to "explore another part of my identity and heritage that I've never experienced through sport".

She said it also offers her "greater high-performance support and the potential to build a more sustainable career" because the "financial and practical challenges of swimming were among the reasons my previous career became difficult to sustain".

Smith has previously said that financial worries, which included a dispute with Swimming South Africa over prize money, contributed to her decision to step away from the sport.

Swimming South Africa has been approached for comment.

Smith won four world and Commonwealth gold medals between 2018 and 2023.

She won gold in the 200m breaststroke and 100m silver at the Tokyo Olympics in 2021, before adding 100m gold and 200m silver in 2024.

"Representing South Africa was a childhood dream come true, and it's a chapter I will always look back on with immense pride and gratitude," she said on social media., external

"The people, the memories and everything we achieved together will always be part of me. But dreams can change as we grow.

"This next chapter is about embracing another part of who I am, falling in love with the journey and discovering what more I'm capable of.

"I'm excited for what lies ahead, and I truly hope you'll continue to be part of the journey with me."

Smith, who said she will continue to live and train in South Africa, is set to represent the Netherlands for the first time at the short course World Championships from 1-6 December.

She acknowledged that her switch "may disappoint or break the hearts" of some supporters.

The Ministry of Sport, Arts and Culture in South Africa said it was aware of the "problems at Swimming South Africa".

"The country was delighted to see her return to the pool, but South Africa does not own her," a statement read.

"Tatjana's choice belongs to her and it should not be turned into a political weapon."

Gayton McKenzie, Minister of Sports, Arts and Culture, said: "Every medal, every record and every anthem she gave us belongs to our history forever. We now have to share her with the Netherlands."

#65. One Nation Victoria candidate apologises for slew of offensive comments about lesbians, Indians and Julia Gillard

Exclusive: Chris Moore, running for the party in Greenvale, made derogatory social media comments between 2010 and 2012

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

A One Nation candidate in Victoria has apologised for a series of offensive comments including repeated references to Australian-Indians as “curry munchers”, comparing a woman in burqa to a bag of rubbish, and sharing an image calling Julia Gillard a slut.

The party’s candidate for Greenvale on Melbourne’s north-western fringe, Chris Moore, also made derogatory comments about lesbians and refugees and once posted that he “doesn’t like the way the new homo cleaner at work looks at me”.

One Nation Victoria has not disendorsed the candidate over the comments, which were made on his personal Facebook page between 2010 and 2012.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

In a statement, Moore told Guardian Australia “these were unthoughtful posts I shared as a young man among close friends”.

“They don’t reflect who I am today,” Moore said. “Today I’m focused on doing the right thing by the voters of Greenvale, and giving them a strong voice in the Victorian Parliament.”

In 2012, Moore shared a picture of a woman and a child both wearing a burqa and standing between two full plastic rubbish bags. He then posted: “Haha spot the difference.”

Moore also shared an image of Gillard and the Home and Away character Alf Stewart. The image of Gillard was accompanied by the caption: “smash my box Alf!” The image of Stewart was accompanied with: “If I wanted a greasy red box I’d go to KFC ya slut.”

Moore also posted saying he “should probably stop shouting ‘Curry Muncher’ at the TV when I’ve got Indians living next door”. He also posted that he “wonders who does the dishes in a lesbian relationship?”

In 2010 he posted: “Just got the curry munchers to wash his car and pretty happy with the way it came up, looks like the boat people are good for something after all?”

Greenvale is a fairly safe Labor seat with a margin of 7.1%. It is held by Iwan Walters, a parliamentary secretary for multicultural affairs.

The electorate has a large Islamic and West Asian population. It has one of the highest proportions of people born in the Middle East and the lowest share of people with no religion.

At the last election, there was a swing of almost 15% against Labor on a two-party preferred basis.

A Liberal party campaign spokesperson said: “Mr Moore will be last on our how-to-vote card in Greenvale.”

A Labor spokesperson said the posts were “reprehensible” and called on One Nation to disendorse the candidate and for the Liberals not to preference him.

“There is no place for racist, Islamophobic and sexist comments,” the Labor spokesperson said.

On Tuesday, One Nation Victoria disendorsed its candidate for Preston, Franco Guardiani, whose private social media account described Adolf Hitler as the “GOAT” and claimed that “importing Islam is making pedophilia legal”.

The party’s Victorian leader, Warren Pickering, said Guardiani was disendorsed as soon as the comments were confirmed.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

“At a time when Jewish Australians are experiencing unprecedented persecution, we are appalled that these comments have been made,” Pickering said.

Guardiani said he was disappointed by the party’s decision to dump him but understood their decision.

One Nation also disendorsed its candidate for the seat of Melbourne, Matthew Collins, over now-deleted comments on social media regarding a number of issues including sexual consent and pornographic jokes. Collins has apologised for the comments.

It is unclear how these candidate’s personal social media histories were not detected during candidate selection and vetting processes. The party has claimed to have invested time and money into improving this process.

In Victoria, One Nation candidates must provide their personal social media accounts for vetting. They are also interviewed by a three-person selection panel including Pickering and two party officials.

The Victorian Liberals also disendorsed the candidate for Williamstown, Paul Lindemann, after becoming aware of social media posts he had made that were offensive towards Indigenous people.

The party had been under pressure by Labor earlier on Wednesday to drop him after the Age revealed he received a six week suspension as a junior basketball coach after allegedly calling an under 14s player “crap”.

“This decision follows comments and views expressed by Mr Lindemann about Indigenous Australians that have recently come to light and were not previously known to the party,” a campaign spokesperson said.

“These comments are offensive, unacceptable and inconsistent with the values and standards expected of Liberal candidates.”

The posts, seen by Guardian Australia, were on an account named after Lindemann’s yacht and featured photos of him and his family. In them, he describes First Nations people as the “most primitive homosapiens on earth” and perpetuated racist tropes about “half-breed” and “full-blood” Indigenous Australians.

Losing Lindemann just weeks out from the 28 November poll is blow to the party, which had hopes of winning the western suburbs seat from Labor, despite it previously being considered a safe Labor seat.

A Labor spokesperson said: “Jess Wilson stood by Paul Lindemann when it came out he harassed and intimidated a girl at under 14s community basketball.

“These were disgusting and offensive posts and this individual should be expelled from the Liberal party.”

#64. US warns Madagascar over growing military ties with Russia

Madagascar has signed defence deals with Russia since the military seized power a year ago

The US has warned Madagascar against strengthening its ties with Russia, saying similar partnerships with military-led governments in West Africa have failed to improve security.

US Africa Command deputy head Lt Gen John Brennan said Madagascar's strategic location in the Indian Ocean made it important to global trade.

He warned against what he called the "Sahel model", which he said risked fuelling instability across the continent.

Mali, Burkina Faso and Niger, all under military rule, have reduced ties with Western partners and turned to Russia to support their fight against jihadist groups.

Russia has expanded its co-operation with Madagascar since the army seized power in 2025, supplying weapons and military instructors.

"We are concerned about Russia wanting to proliferate [the Sahel model] elsewhere across the continent," Brennan told reporters in an online briefing.

"Madagascar sits in a location that is extremely important, as is Mozambique, and we don't want to see those models be applied in those two locations," he said.

The three Sahel countries had blamed their former Western partners for failing to contain jihadist violence before turning to Moscow.

However, Islamist militant attacks have continued despite their closer military ties with Russia.

Brennan also raised concerns about security in the Mozambique Channel, which separates Mozambique and Madagascar from mainland Africa and is an important route for international shipping and trade.

He said it was vital to prevent militants linked to the Islamist State group in northern Mozambique and other hostile actors from threatening maritime activity.

Citing recent piracy-related incidents along the coast, Brennan said they were adapting their tactics, including developing greater maritime capabilities.

He said US partners were "free to partner with whom they wish" but urged them to understand the potential risks.

Madagascar has not publicly responded to Brennan's comments.

However, President MichaÃ«l Randrianirina has recently reiterated his support for stronger ties between Russia and African countries.

During the Russia-Africa summit last month, Madagascar and Russia signed several co-operation agreements covering defence, tourism and trade.

In February, a few months after seizing power, Randrianirina visited to Moscow. It was the first official visit to Russia by a Malagasy leader in nearly 50 years.

#61. 'France has some of the cleanest electricity in the world, but we're not using enough of it'

Eve Irvine is pleased to welcome Thierry Bros, energy & climate expert and Professor of Science and Economics at Sciences Po. As geopolitical conflicts unsettle global energy markets and governments struggle to reconcile climate commitments with economic and security imperatives, the answer lies in a fundamental reconsideration of the sequence of climate policy. "Electrification first," he argues, acknowledging that this "doesn't necessarily mean clean electricity." His argument is grounded in a critical assessment of international climate diplomacy and European energy policy. "We've seen many COPs, and they've all failed," he contends, questioning the effectiveness of ambitious targets that have yet to deliver the necessary structural transformation. In his view, electrification offers a more pragmatic route than some of the technological priorities previously championed by European policymakers.

#59. Egyptian journalist's retrial adjourned as UN warns of 'assaults on press freedom'

Lina Attalah has said Egypt is "trying to limit the already-limited space" for independent media

A criminal court in Cairo has postponed the retrial of the prominent Egyptian journalist Lina Attalah, whose case has drawn international condemnation.

Wednesday's opening hearing was adjourned until 18 November after defence lawyers representing the editor-in-chief of the news outlet Mada Masr requested a new judge.

The UN has warned of "widening assaults on press freedom by Egyptian authorities, including the targeting, persecution and harassment of independent journalists".

Attalah told the BBC that a recent crackdown was meant to "send a chilling message" to "the very few newsrooms that are left".

She warned: "Everybody should be careful, everybody should be scared, everybody should be wondering if they are next."

Set up 13 years ago, Mada Masr became Egypt's leading independent news site with Attalah as its editor-in-chief and co-founder.

Last month, the New Cairo Misdemeanours Court convicted her of running a website without a licence - which she said she had tried in vain for years to obtain. She was issued a hefty fine of 3 million Egyptian pounds ($56,000; Â£42,000) and Mada Masr was ordered to shut down.

After that trial, Attalah found that a court had previously given her a one-year prison sentence, without her knowledge, for "spreading false news" - a charge often used against journalists and dissidents - as well as operating a website without a licence. It was issued in absentia in 2024, even though she was in the country at the time. Attalah is now being retried in that case by the same judge.

At the opening session, Attalah's defence team requested the judge's recusal, arguing that he had "already formed a conviction in mind against the defendant", Mada Masr cited its lawyer Hassan al-Azhar as saying, external. The judge went into recess before adjourning proceedings for a week to allow the court to consider the defence's request, he added.

Mada Masr also said Attalah's appeal against last month's conviction was scheduled to take place on 25 November.

Egypt has largely stifled freedom of the press, ranking 169th out of 180 countries for press freedom, according to Reporters Without Borders' 2026 analysis. The group says that at least 26 journalists who were arrested in recent years remain in detention.

While most newspapers and television stations now keep to government lines, Mada Masr remained a rare, independent and investigative news source in Egypt. However, since 2017 the site could only be viewed from outside the country.

It had reported on alleged corruption and human rights abuses, and provided deep coverage of politics and economics, as well as regional upheaval and wars in Libya, Sudan and Gaza.

In her BBC interview on Sunday, Attalah insisted that the factual reporting and professional standards of Mada Masr had an important role to play in Egyptian society, which could benefit the government.

"It's much better if you have a little space left to have it open for professional media organisations who do rigorous reporting, because if we disappear, then you make the space for conspiracies and disinformation," she said.

"People need to know, people need to consume information and you're shooting yourself in the foot if you're killing the newsrooms that do it properly."

In September, six journalists with the independent fact-checking platform, Matsda2sh - constituting its entire newsroom - were arrested in home raids by the Egyptian authorities and held incommunicado for nearly a week.

Six journalists working for the independent fact-checking platform Matsda2sh are facing terrorism charges

They have since been charged with joining the Muslim Brotherhood and financing terrorism, and their lawyers report that several were beaten and electrocuted in detention. They say that no evidence has been presented linking them to the Brotherhood.

Egypt's former president Mohammed Morsi â who was backed by the Muslim Brotherhood â was removed from power by the military, led by Abdul Fattah al-Sisi, in July 2013 after widespread protests. Under Sisi, who became president the following year, the Islamist movement was classed as a terrorist organisation, leading to a widespread clampdown.

On Tuesday, a spokesperson for the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights, Thameen Al-Kheetan, called for the Matsda2sh reporters to be released, with all charges dropped.

He also said the case against Attalah "raises fresh concerns regarding due process and fair trial".

"We call on Egypt to end all forms of harassment of journalists and media outlets, and to respect and fulfil the right to freedom of expression, including the right to information," Al-Kheetan said.

"The steps taken to suppress the continued work of both Matsda2sh and Mada Masr must be overturned. The people of Egypt have a right to read and hear views expressed through these fora on issues of legitimate public concern."

Attalah has suggested the authorities might again be trying to silence independent voices at a time of economic strain in Egypt and regional instability.

It has been widely noted that recent legal action against journalists comes after the US State Department waived human rights conditions on $320m (Â£240m) in its foreign military funding for Egypt in September. This allowed the funds to be released as part of Washington's annual $1.3bn military aid package.

The BBC asked US Secretary of State Marco Rubio whether he had raised the recent crackdown on independent journalism with Egypt's Foreign Minister, Badr Abdelatty, at their meeting about regional tensions in Paris on Monday.

"We didn't discuss it today," Rubio said. "Butâ¦ like anything else in foreign policy, there's always a balancing act to further the national interest of the United States. And in the end, Egypt has been a reliable and good defence partner of the United States."

"That doesn't mean you agree with everything another government does," he went on, adding that he was not "opining about their journalists".

#57. NZ election 2026: New Zealand faces weeks of uncertainty with results ‘too close to call’

National party will be largest in parliament but ultimate shape of next government won’t be settled for some time

New Zealand could be facing a weeks-long wait to know the shape of its next government, after its nail-biting election failed to hand a decisive victory to either the incumbent rightwing coalition or opposition left bloc.

On preliminary results, the centre-right National party, led by the prime minister, Christopher Luxon, managed to secure enough seats to govern alongside its traditional coalition partners, the libertarian Act and populist New Zealand First parties.

But with some seats won by very slim margins, and special votes yet to be counted, that success is precarious, resulting in an election too close to call until official results are released on 27 November.

Special votes include overseas votes and ballots cast outside a voter’s electorate. In 2023, 600,000 such votes were made, 21% of the total. These votes tend to favour the left – in 2023, special votes resulted in Te Pāti Māori (the Māori party) and the Greens gaining three seats between them.

With nearly 100% of the regular votes counted on Saturday night, National secured 42 seats, Act 13 and New Zealand First 12. The combined 67-seat majority is wafer thin, with the left bloc just three seats behind.

Speaking to a room full of the party faithful at Auckland’s Viaduct events centre just before midnight, Luxon said the results were “too close to call” and the special votes would need to be counted before negotiations could begin.

National was still the largest party in parliament, he said, and had “a pathway to forming the next government”. National pipped Labour by 0.02% in the party vote.

Labour, led by Chris Hipkins, secured 35 seats, while its traditional coalition partners the Greens won 16 and Te Pāti Māori five. Even with the seven seats from the centrist newcomer party, Opportunity, the left bloc is three seats short of forming a government.

Before a rowdy crowd of supporters at the Cosmopolitan Club in Upper Hutt, north of Wellington, Hipkins joked that New Zealand had spoken but “it may take a bit longer to work out what they’ve said”.

“As things stand tonight, it is clear that we are not going to know the shape of our next government for a little while longer yet,” Hipkins said, promising that Labour would get “straight to work” should the final count land in its favour.

The collapse of the two major parties, amid a sluggish economy and stubbornly high ⁠inflation, is feeding record support for the smaller parties, including Act, New Zealand First and the Greens. It is also helped propel Opportunity into parliament for the first time.

Opinion polls in the lead-up to the election had Opportunity placed to become the kingmaker. At this stage, the right bloc does not need the party to govern, but if special votes change the landscape, its predicted role could come to fruition.

Currently at 5.9%, the centrist party has met the threshold needed to enter parliament, a historic first. Since New Zealand adopted the mixed-member proportional (MMP) electoral system in 1996, no new party has been able to meet that threshold – all the other minor parties have been born from MPs or groups splintering from larger parties.

The party’s leader, Qiulae Wong, told her supporters in Auckland she was “stoked”.

“It’s looking very promising for us,” she said. “Let’s just see how the rest of the votes fall.”

In New Zealand, voters cast two votes: one for an electorate MP and one for a party. The system is meant to produce a parliament that reflects each party’s share of the nationwide vote.

However, if New Zealanders vote for more electorate MPs from a particular party than their party vote would entitle them to, they get to keep the extra MPs in what is called an overhang.

National won more seats that its party support allows for, meaning it could gain an extra five seats to make up the balance.

That will be good news for its path to power, but comes with some significant trade-offs for the party. Four of its most senior ministers, including Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith, Chris Bishop and Gerry Brownlee, will not return to parliament because they did not win a seat, or the party didn’t win enough votes to bring in list-only MPs.

On the other side of the house, Te Pāti Māori picked up five overhang seats.

With all the extra seats, there could be 130 in the next parliament – a record under MMP – which means 65 seats are needed to form a coalition.

The country faces a two-week wait until special votes are announced, followed by the possibility of weeks of negotiations. Luxon said caretaker government conventions would apply in the meantime.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well-established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

#55. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#53. Judge allows Trump's $10bn defamation case against BBC to proceed

A federal judge in Florida has allowed US President Donald Trump's defamation case against the BBC to go ahead after the broadcaster sought to dismiss it.

But Judge Jeffrey Kuntz dismissed a second claim in the $10bn (Â£7.5bn) lawsuit under the state's unfair trade practices law.

The US president is suing the BBC over an episode of Panorama that edited together two different sections of a speech he gave before the riot at the US Capitol in 2021.

Trump called the judge's ruling a "huge victory" in a Truth Social post on Tuesday. A BBC spokesperson said: "We are surprised by this decision but respect the Court's authority and note the case will now proceed to the next stage."

The BBC spokesperson added: "We remain confident in our position and will continue to respond through the proper legal process."

Judge Kuntz found Trump's defamation allegations against the BBC sufficient for the case to continue, although he did not make a ruling on the merits of the case.

"Whether President Trump can prove these claims and establish he sustained damages at summary judgment or trial is left for another day," he wrote in Tuesday's order.

Trump's lawsuit - filed in Florida - accuses the BBC of "intentionally, maliciously and deceptively doctoring" the speech he gave outside the Capitol on 6 January 2021.

The BBC apologised and said that the broadcast had given "the mistaken impression that Trump made a direct call for violent action".

The broadcaster has since asked that the court dismiss the lawsuit, arguing the programme does not meet the legal criteria for defamation and was not available in the US.

Along with defamation, Trump claimed in the lawsuit that the broadcaster had violated Florida law, specifically the Deceptive and Unfair Trade Practices Act.

Kuntz on Tuesday dismissed that part of the lawsuit, ruling that the documentary did not constitute commercial speech.

Trump ordered to answer questions under oath in BBC lawsuit

A judge ordered Trump last month to answer questions under oath in the lawsuit by 11 December, after the president cancelled a previous deposition last month, citing "matters of national importance".

A lawyer representing the BBC said in court that Trump was campaigning in Georgia in October on the scheduled day and had "prioritised that" over answering their questions.

Earlier this year, Trump's lawyers conceded they had no evidence that the documentary was available in the US on the BBC's BritBox subscription service - as they originally argued - or on the BBC's website, BBC Select or through US broadcasters.

Trump ultimately agreed to drop BBC Studios, the broadcaster's commercial arm, from his defamation lawsuit, after the BBC argued that it had "no role in creating or producing the documentary".

#51. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#50. Uyghurs facing deportation by Thailand say they will be ‘like slaves’ in China

Activists fear Bangkok will give in to pressure from Beijing despite reports of torture and human rights abuses against the mostly muslim ethnic minority

The deportation of two more Uyghurs to China from Thailand would put their lives in grave danger, activists have said, with one of the men saying he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back.

Last year, Thai authorities forcibly deported 40 Uyghur men to China despite pleas from the men themselves and the UN. The US and Canada had reportedly offered to resettle them, but Bangkok appeared to want to avoid upsetting Beijing. The UN has said the returnees’ whereabouts remain unknown.

Now, two more men fear becoming the latest victims of Beijing’s repressive crackdown against the largely Muslim ethnic group, condemned by the UN as a potential crime against humanity.

The girlfriend of one of the men said her boyfriend, whose name is being withheld for security reasons, told the Guardian he wanted to go “anywhere else but China”.

Human rights groups said two Uyghur men were at risk of deportation after recently being transferred to an immigration detention centre and they feared Thai authorities may once again give in to pressure from Beijing.

Elaine Pearson, Asia director at Human Rights Watch, called for the men to be allowed to travel to a safe third country. “Previous Thai governments have kept Uyghurs in inhumane detention for years and then complied with Beijing’s demands to send them to China, where they were forcibly disappeared,” she said.

The men had been serving a seven-year prison sentence for offences related to an attempted escape from immigration detention in January 2020. They were part of a group of more than 200 Uyghur men, women and children who were apprehended by Thai police in 2014 near the border with Malaysia after they fled from China.

Since 2014, women and children in the group have been released to Turkey, while the men have been deported to China, in separate batches, attracting criticism from the United Nations and the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio.

Uyghurs, a Turkic minority mainly from the Xinjiang region in China, have been subjected to decades of oppressive and discriminatory treatment from the Chinese authorities. This has included the incarceration of hundreds of thousands of people in detention camps aimed at stamping out their cultural identity.

Beijing has defended its policies in Xinjiang as being aimed at poverty alleviation and counter-terrorism.

In July, Marzeyya Kasem’s (who asked to be referred to by her Islamic alias) boyfriend wrote to her in a letter that he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back to China. “Sometimes I thought why I’m a human being. What I do wrong against China … no want to go back to China,” he wrote. She shared the letter with a Thai rights group.

The two men facing imminent deportation reportedly pleaded their case to Thailand’s office of the National Human Rights Commission on Wednesday.

Relatives of Uyghurs have said the Chinese government was “paranoid about Uyghurs going to a safe country in large numbers and living together”, and preferred to have them deported.

It is not clear whether China has issued a formal extradition request for the two men. But Chaipruak Doungprapat, Thailand’s national security council chief, told reporters that the men, along with three other Uyghurs who are still in prison, had arrest warrants outstanding in China.

Some have questioned whether criminal warrants could be credible considering that at least one of them, Kasem’s boyfriend, was a minor when he left China. “If they are really wanted for a crime in China, why didn’t China immediately issue a [Interpol] red notice [when they were detained in 2014]?” asked Kasem.

Kasem believes that because Thailand was criticised for last year’s mass deportation, this time the authorities were trying to establish a more credible legal basis for the move.

A spokesperson for China’s foreign affairs ministry said: “The cooperation between China and Thailand in combating cross-border illegal and criminal activities such as smuggling is consistent with the laws of the two countries, international law and international practice.”

A spokesperson for Thailand’s foreign affairs ministry declined to comment.

They are demanding a relief from surging diesel prices, which have been driven up by the war in Iran.

Talks between government officials and protest leaders have so far failed to reach a resolution, with another meeting expected to take place on Wednesday.

The protest is being organised by the â Fuerza del Norte trade union, which represents around 80% of Chile's lorry drivers, and the No+TAG civic protest âmovement.

"There is no point of agreement with the government that could offer any perspective to find solutions for everyone," AndrÃ©s AlarcÃ³n, a spokesman for No+TAG, said after talks broke down on Tuesday, adding "the strike continues".

He stressed the protests were impacting economic activity and exports, saying "we need to get the country moving again".

The government of President JosÃ© Antonio Kast has faced public discontent over fuel prices for months.

"Right now I'm not making any profit. Sometimes you even have to put in your own money to cover the client," AFP quoted the 47-year-old as saying.

#45. Nvidia opens investigation after report CEO Jensen Huang was target of foreign intelligence

CEO reportedly ‌had close relationship ‌with a Chinese businesswoman who passed information to ‌Beijing

Nvidia said ⁠on Sunday it “is initiating an independent investigation in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort”.

The New York Times reported in ​October that Donald Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence that Huang ‌had a close relationship ‌with a Chinese businesswoman who passed information to ‌Beijing.

Speaking to reporters on Sunday, Trump downplayed the revelations about Huang, who is an influential adviser to the president on artificial intelligence, saying: “I think he’s an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it.”

Trump also brushed off a question about whether he now regrets not acting on the intelligence when he was briefed on it, and suggested that he would be happy to take Huang with him on a trip to Asia next week. “I haven’t heard he’s done anything wrong,” Trump said. “He dealt with somebody. But, you know, so does everybody else.”

Republican lawmakers have expressed more alarm, asking Huang to give the House foreign affairs and intelligence committees a closed-door briefing next week on Nvidia’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage” and his relationship with Alice Huang or Huang Le, an executive at a Singapore-based datacenter company called Megaspeed, who is of no relation to the Nvidia CEO. She had previously been suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, prompting American officials to surveil and monitor her.

Those officials have determined that she was acting as a Chinese intelligence asset, passing commercial information to Beijing, the New York Times reported, citing unnamed sources.

The company’s board said in a statement on Sunday that, while it has no reason to believe that either US national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, its inquiry will examine all available information.

“The Board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of NVIDIA and its shareholders,” the company said. “The Board has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team and will provide further updates as appropriate.”

Huang fully ‌supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it, it added.

Axios reported on ​Sunday, citing a draft email by Huang to employees, that Huang admitted to having a romantic relationship with a suspected Chinese spy and took full ⁠responsibility for his actions.

“I was unfaithful to my ​wife. My ​actions hurt her ​and our children. There is no excuse ​for what ‌I did,” ​Huang said ​in the draft email also seen by Reuters.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Axios report.

The Times reported on Sunday that a memo drafted by US secretary of state Marco Rubio circulated among top policymakers in the Trump administration last spring, citing intelligence that the businesswoman who had a relationship with Nvidia’s chief executive was a spy for China’s ministry of state security. Despite those concerns, Huang was invited to join Trump’s delegation when he traveled to China in May. Huang and his wife, Lori Huang, were also invited to a state dinner at the White House in September for Xi Jinping and seated at the top table with Trump and China’s president.

Nvidia’s powerful chips serve as a cornerstone of the AI boom, placing Jensen Huang into one of the most influential leadership positions in the industry. In that position, he has developed a close partnership with Trump as the US government attempts to defend its position as a leader in AI development.

However, national security concerns have prompted strict US export controls, effectively barring the company from selling its top-tier processors to customers in China.

US officials have cited threats to the US supply chain and fears over national security risks as a rationale for the restrictions. Scott Bessent, the US treasury secretary, has also suggested that China could be sanctioned for theft of US data and intellectual property.

The Trump administration has imposed restrictions on exports of advanced AI chips to China as a matter of national security. Megaspeed’s Alice Huang was suspected to have been helping the government get around those restrictions.

The investigation also follows a rough few days on Wall Street for AI tech firms, particularly Nvidia. A sell-off of tech stocks that began earlier this month and gained steam with disappointing earnings led to a total decline of nearly $1tn in value off the tech-heavy Nasdaq exchange in one week and erased about $2tn in a month.

A drop of 3.1% for Nvidia, previously worth about $5tn, whose chips have made it the poster child of the AI frenzy, was the heaviest force pulling the S&P 500 downward recently.

Nick Robins-Early and Blake Montgomery contributed reporting

#42. Kennedys sue to block Trump's arch, arguing it would cast shadow on JFK's grave

An artist's rendering of Trump's proposed arch near Arlington Cemetery

The family of former US President John F Kennedy has sued to block construction of a triumphal arch that President Donald Trump plans to build in the nation's capital.

Nine members of the political dynasty argue the 250ft (76m) structure would block the view of Kennedy's grave, as well as the graves of thousands of veterans buried at Arlington National Cemetery in Virginia.

Last week, a US judge temporarily halted the project on the heels of a vote allowing construction to proceed.

The White House said it was "unfortunate" to see that some members of the Kennedy family had joined litigation against an arch "intended to honor our heroes who rest in Arlington Cemetery".

"The judiciary should not be abused to politicize a structure designed to unite our nation," White House spokesman Davis R Ingle said in a statement to the BBC.

"We will continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds," he added.

Watch: Protesters demonstrate against Trumpâs proposed arch

Last week the project won approval from the National Capital Planning Commission.

But US District Judge Tanya Chutkan then ruled there should be a 14-day pause before any work can start on the arch as litigation plays out.

The Kennedy family filed its complaint under a joint lawsuit brought by the state of Virginia and Washington DC seeking to block construction, arguing that the arch will damage "historically significant sightlines" if erected at the gateway of the cemetery.

The plaintiffs include seven children of Robert and Ethel Kennedy, as well as their grandson - former Democrat congressman Joe Kennedy - and Ted Kennedy's son Edward Kennedy Jr.

It does not include Trump cabinet official Robert F Kennedy Jr, who leads the Department of Health and Human Services.

Their motion moves to intervene in two other lawsuits filed by conservationists and veterans seeking to halt the construction.

They say the arch would be a "permanent denigration" of the gravesites and that the Kennedy offspring "would never again be able to appreciate both sites as they always have before, and as the Kennedy family always intended".

The Kennedy family argues the structure "will permanently cast shadows over and damage historically significant sightlines to the existing gravesites and memorials established in the Kennedy family's honor" at the cemetery.

The structure would also "fundamentally alter the experience of visiting the gravesites", including the memorials of hundreds of thousands of US veterans, the Kennedys said.

The complaint said that the National Park Service and Interior Department authorised construction on 5 November 2026 without the necessary congressional approval.

The so-called Arc de Trump is one of the president's efforts to remake the capital city and is meant to commemorate the country's 250th anniversary.

Last year, he also added his name to the Kennedy Center, a major Washington DC cultural institution named in honour of JFK.

Trump's name remained on the facade for six months until the addition was ruled illegal and removed.

#41. US and rest of world must follow China’s lead on green energy, says John Kerry

Former US secretary of state says most nations are ‘stumbling along’ while Beijing has ‘hugely accelerated’

China is leading the world in moving away from fossil fuels, and the rest of the globe – including the US – must now follow, the former US secretary of state John Kerry has told the Guardian, as Cop watchers wonder exactly how China is going to approach this year’s climate summit.

As Cop31 opens – this year the climate conference is in Turkey – there is plenty of speculation about whether China will finally step into the leadership role that it has long avoided. In its latest “five-year plan” adopted in March 2026, China for the first time pledged to “actively participate in and lead global climate governance”. Cop31 could provide the first indications Beijing is ready to put that rhetoric into action, something it has historically been reluctant to do.

Green energy is a vital component of the export engine that has kept China’s sluggish post-pandemic economy on track. The success of its exports stands in contrast to its failure to significantly boost domestic general consumption, a structural weakness that has persistently vexed leaders.

In 2025, clean-tech exports surpassed $220bn, and they should grow again this year; the world’s largest emitter is now the world’s leading supplier of clean energy technology. China accounts for about 85% of solar and 80% of lithium-ion battery supply chain production capacity, and in 2025, nearly 75% of electric cars were manufactured there.

Smart moves now by China would position it as a linchpin in a future electrostate world order, persuade rivals to cooperate, and solidify its influence in the global south.

Meanwhile other countries, including the US under Donald Trump, have fallen badly behind. Kerry said they would lose out economically – as well as putting the climate in further danger – unless they, too, embraced low-carbon power and rapid electrification. “The only country that has hugely accelerated is China. The rest of the world is stumbling along while Mother Nature is sending us more powerful signals every week, every day,” he said.

“[China] realised suddenly they had way too much dependency on oil and gas from other places, and they decided they needed to be independent. Other countries, since the war in Iran, and since the strait of Hormuz has been interrupted, have made a similar decision, and they’re moving independently. There’s been a massive uptick in deployment of renewables. Thank you, Donald Trump.”

For autocratic states without free and fair elections, seeing the economic benefits reaped by China would be the spur to adopt low-carbon growth, he added.

“They’ll see where the market is going, and they will recognise where the cheaper alternatives are. Even they can read a spreadsheet, and understand where costs are exorbitant [in fossil fuels], and where you have a better deal,” Kerry said. “Right now, renewables together with storage, together with some transitional gas, is cheaper and more effective, than just going with fossil fuels.”

He added: “The marketplace has made its decision and globally is moving to electrons, and electrostate status. People are going to electricity and they’re going to try to do that as clean as humanly possible. That’s the choice that I see being implemented in many different places. It is unstoppable.”

China has already said it wants Cop31 to discuss the free flow of green technology against a backdrop of “intensifying” geopolitical conflicts – widely seen as a reference to US and EU tariffs on items such as Chinese electric vehicles and solar products.

Li Shuo from the Asia Society Policy Institute (ASPI) puts it this way: “In a world where climate action was once seen as a constraint on growth but is now increasingly a driver of it, China faces a singular question: will it step into a leadership role not out of obligation, but out of self-interest?”

The summit’s presidency has promised this Cop will be one of “implementation and acceleration” – and when it comes to the green transition, nowhere has implemented or accelerated quite like China. As a result, emissions have been in a “flat or falling” trend since March 2024, analysis for Carbon Brief shows.

Beijing sees the energy transition as “central to economic modernisation and energy security, with significant implications for international influence”, said Kevin Tu from the Oxford Institute for Energy Studies. The wars in Ukraine and the Middle East and their effect on oil and gas supplies have only hardened that stance.

The conflicts have also highlighted the geopolitical implications of a planetary shift to green energy. In the fossil fuel era, the designation of the US dollar as the global trading currency for oil gave Washington “what has been termed the exorbitant privilege of being the global lender of last resort”, said Isabel Hilton, the founder of the specialist environment outlet China Dialogue.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“Without that, the US position in the world looks weaker, and if you add China’s dominance of the supply of clean technologies and the supply chains that produce them, US dominance looks more vulnerable still.”

Meanwhile, President Xi Jinping describes China as a “doer and go-getter working for the cause of global development” and state media frequently showcase Chinese renewable projects in countries ranging from Indonesia to Pakistan and Kenya. The value of the green energy deals in China’s belt and road initiative in the first half of 2026 has already surpassed the total for the whole of 2025, a recent analysis showed.

Those countries could be useful allies if China can get the subject of tariffs into the talks. “China is likely to find support from Brazil, India, South Africa and other developing countries concerned about the costs and market-access effects of climate-related trade measures,” said Tu.

China has generally held back from policy ambition or agenda-setting at forums such as Cop. Its attitude to the plans to set a global target for electrification has been ambivalent, even though it would easily hit it, according to ASPI’s Kate Logan. Partly, China has an aversion to prescriptive outside targets, but it is also trying to navigate the divergent interests of its global south partners.

Yixian Sun, a professor of sustainability governance at the University of Bath, said: “I still think there is a mismatch between Chinese policymakers’ views on the role that China can play in global climate governance and international expectations on the influence that China should exert.”

If the world wants China to play a leadership role, the environment needs to change, and more trust extended, he says. “They have to be ready to accept China’s leadership, and I don’t think this is the case, especially for global north countries”.

Analysts will watch China’s actions around climate finance commitments and support for a “just transition” – a framework designed to secure workers’ rights and livelihoods in the move away from fossil fuels.

They will also be looking for signals Beijing will bid for Cop33 in 2028; officials are said to be trying to decide if hosting would be beneficial.

For now, said Logan, “China would be happy with a Cop that doesn’t necessarily produce anything monumental, but comes out with a relatively cooperative outcome … so no major drama.”
